## Page 1


A Physical space derivation of Morawetz-Energy estimates in
Kerr spacetimes with large angular momentum.
Lili He and Sergiu Klainerman
July 13, 2026
Abstract
We revisit the derivation of Morawetz–energy estimates for scalar wave equations in the domain
of outer communication of a Kerr spacetime K(a, m). Our goal is to develop robust physical-space
methods which are well suited for extension to realistic perturbations of Kerr.
The proof rests on several ingredients. First, we derive conditional Morawetz estimates which
extend the physical-space techniques initiated by Andersson and Blue [2], and later adapted in [18]
to perturbations of slowly rotating Kerr, by exploiting a physical-space characterization of the full
r-range of trapped null geodesics. Second, we use an idea introduced by Stogin [41] in the axially
symmetric case to handle the low-frequency difficulties in the Morawetz estimates. In the general
case, the control of the lower-order terms also requires making full use of the principal trapping term
in the Morawetz bulk norm, together with a new use of Hardy-type inequalities.
A further new ingredient is the control of the boundary terms generated by the Morawetz esti-
mates. This is based on two additional ideas: physical-space versions of Whiting’s transform [44],
developed in a forthcoming paper [19], which yield a flux-independent energy estimate; and an adap-
tation of the Andersson–Blue invariant-operator method, which turns that estimate into a bound
for the horizon flux. Finally, a continuity argument yields an unconditional global-in-time Morawetz
estimate, while a new energy estimate is obtained from the construction of a causal vectorfield which
is Killing on the trapping set.
The results proved here are restricted to scalar wave equations, corresponding to spin 0, in the
range |a|/m ≤0.75. We expect this restriction to be technical, and the methods developed in this
paper to extend to the Teukolsky equation.

1
Introduction
1.1
General Motivation
This is the first in a projected sequence of papers whose principal goal is to revisit the derivation of
Morawetz–energy estimates for wave equations on Kerr spacetimes K(a, m), in the full subextremal
range |a| < m, using robust physical-space methods. Since such estimates are principally motivated by
the Kerr black-hole stability problem, we begin by recalling the present status of the conjecture.1
The first complete nonlinear stability result for the Kerr family was obtained, for |a|/m ≪1, in the
sequence of works [24], [25], [26], [18], and [38]. As observed already in [26], the smallness assumption
on the angular momentum enters only in [18], mainly through the derivation of decay estimates for
the Teukolsky equations satisfied by the extreme curvature components. The general strategy for such
estimates combines Morawetz–energy estimates with rp-weighted estimates.
For the exact Kerr spacetime K(a, m), Morawetz–energy estimates in the full subextremal range |a| < m
are already known: see [13] for the scalar wave equation, and [36], [37], [33] for the Teukolsky equation.
These results rely in an essential way on frequency analysis and on Whiting’s deep mode stability theorem
[44]. For this reason, they appeared difficult to adapt directly to realistic perturbations of Kerr, of the type
expected in a proof of nonlinear stability. This difficulty has recently been overcome by Ma and Szeftel
in [31] and [32], where they derive full energy–Morawetz estimates for both the scalar wave equation and
the Teukolsky equation on realistic perturbations of subextremal Kerr, using the results of [13] and [33]
as black boxes together with delicate microlocal techniques in the spirit of [42] and [28].
It now appears that, by combining these advances with the methods developed for the nonlinear stability
of slowly rotating Kerr, especially the gauge constructions in [26], [24], [25], and [38], the remaining con-
ceptual obstacles to the full subextremal Kerr stability conjecture have largely been removed. Although
we can no longer aspire to be the first to provide a proof for the missing piece, we believe that the
physical-space methods developed here and in our forthcoming paper [19] remain valuable. They offer a
way to simplify, unify, and clarify aspects of how to derive decay of waves in both Kerr and perturbations
of Kerr, which otherwise remains technically involved and dependent on several sophisticated black boxes.
We also expect these methods to be useful in other problems in general relativity, and possibly beyond.
As we were finishing this paper, we became aware of a recent work of Hintz [20] which also claims to
settle the nonlinear stability of the full subextremal Kerr family. Though there are major differences
between the two approaches, one can make a few comparisons between the results. Hintz works with an
unconventional class of initial data, consisting of a structured, polyhomogeneous, more slowly decaying
part, together with a general remainder term with O(r−3−ϵ) decay.
In the traditional framework of
weighted Ck or Hs spaces, only the O(r−3−ϵ) component is generic; in this sense the result is more
restrictive than the O(r−3/2−ϵ) assumptions used in [26], [18], and [32].2
In the language of the null-frame curvature formalism, Hintz’s result for the core O(r−3−ϵ) component
is consistent with full peeling, namely O(r−5) and O(r−4) decay for the α and β curvature components,
rather than the weaker O(r−7/2−ϵ) behavior. The structured, more slowly decaying part of the data,
1For a more complete discussion, see the introductions to [26], [18], and the survey [8].
2This is also the initial-data decay used in the original Christodoulou–Klainerman stability theorem for Minkowski space.
Major improvements on the required decay were obtained by Bieri [3] and by Shen [39], [40]; the latter proves stability of
Minkowski space with only O(r−δ) decay.
6


## Page 7


however, leads to serious violations of peeling and is responsible for some of the main difficulties addressed
in that work.3
On the technical side, Hintz’s proof relies on microlocal methods developed in his long-term collaboration
with A. Vasy. One striking feature of the argument is the reduction of the gauge problem, which is
intrinsically infinite-dimensional, to a finite-dimensional problem for this restricted class of initial data;
see Remark 1.3 in [20]. Finally, it is important to note that the approaches of [32] and [20], despite being
very different, rely on Whiting’s mode stability theorem as an indispensable input.
1.2
Main features of our results and methods
For simplicity of the exposition we limit ourselves in this paper to the case of the standard wave equation
□a,mψ = N. Moreover, for technical reasons, our results are restricted to the case |a|/m ≤0.75.
To start with, our method of deriving Morawetz estimates relies on the commutation properties of the
relevant geometric wave equations in K(a, m) with the second order operators generated by the Killing
vectorfields T, Z and the second Carter operator O. The method, to which we refer broadly, as the
Andersson-Blue (AB) invariant operators method, was first introduced in [2] for solutions of the scalar
wave equation □a,mψ = 0, for slowly rotating Kerr K(a, m), |a|/m ≪1. It was extended in [18] to derive
decay estimates for the extreme Teukolsky variables (part II of [18]) in perturbations of Kerr as well as
to provide control of the top derivatives of the curvature components (part III of [18]). The assumption
of small rotation |a|/m ≪1 was crucially used in [18] both in the derivation of the conditional Morawetz
estimates (chapters 6-9) and the derivation of the energy estimates (section 7.3. of [18]).
Morawetz–energy estimates in the full subextremal range |a| < m are known: see [13] for the scalar wave
equation, and [36]–[37], [33] for the Teukolsky equation. The proof in [13], in particular, relies on three
ingredients closely tied to Carter’s mode separation for the wave equation on Kerr [5]–[6]: the quantitative
mode stability theorem of Shlapentokh-Rothman [35], based on Whiting’s transformation method; the
strict separation between superradiant and trapped frequencies in the full subextremal range; and the
freedom to choose vectorfields adapted to individual mode solutions.
In our work we rely instead on the following new ingredients:
1. A far reaching extension of the Andersson-Blue (AB)–invariant operators method [2] which takes
full advantage of the precise r-range of trapped null geodesics (discussed in section 3).
2. The innovative technique introduced by Stogin in his PhD thesis [41], in the context of axially
symmetric solutions, to deal with the low frequency difficulties of the Morawetz estimates. The
control of the lower order Morawetz bulk term for general solutions requires, in addition, making
full use of the principal trapping term4 of the bulk Morawetz norm, as well as a new way to make
use of Hardy type inequalities.
3. We make use of a direct bound on the energy norm of solutions, independent of having first to
control the flux through the horizon. The bound5, stated in Theorem 1.3 below, is based on a series
3It is known that full peeling is inconsistent with many important physical applications; see, for example, [9], [10], [15],
and [22].
4Typically, in the trapping set, one only uses the non-negativity of the trapping term.
5Conditional on controlling the energy restricted to a compact set.
7


## Page 8


of new, physical space adaptations, of Whiting’s integral transformation technique. We delay a full
discussion of these transformations, as well as the proof of Theorem 1.3, to our forthcoming paper
[19].
4. To derive the crucial bound for the flux along the horizon, stated in Theorem 1.4, we combine
Theorem 1.3 with an adaptation of the AB–invariant operators technique.
5. The estimates of Theorems 1.3 and 1.4 only provide bounds for Tψ. To derive a similar bound for
ψ we need the additional result of Proposition 1.5.
6. A continuity type argument6, which ties together the preceding conditional estimates above (i.e.
conditional on controlling the energy restricted to a compact set) to derive an unconditional global
in time Morawetz estimate.
7. A new energy type estimate based on the construction of a causal vectorfield, in the range |a|/m ≤
0.9, which is Killing in the trapping set.
1.3
Statement of the Main Theorem
We rely on the notations and definitions made in the preliminary section 2 to state our main result. Most
importantly we work with Boyer-Lindquist coordinates (t, r, θ, ϕ), see (2.1), and the principal null pair
(e3, e4, e1, e2) given by (2.16), (2.17) and the two Killing vectorfields T = ∂t, Z = ∂ϕ. In addition the
Kerr metric has a second order symmetry connected to the Carter tensor defined by Lemma 2.1. Thus
the conformal wave operator |q|2□commutes not only with the first order operators T and Z but also
the second order operator O given by Proposition 2.2.
Our estimates for solutions of (1.2) are stated in the domain of outer communication D = {r ≥r+}

in the exterior region D of K(a, m). To state our main results below we refer to section 2.4 where the bulk,
energy and flux quantities B, E, F, the combined quantities BEF, EF, BF, as well as the bulk quantity N

at the horizon, and then passes to the non-degenerate form by an additional red shift inequality9. We
also make use of the rp weighted version of all these quantities10.

Theorem 1.1 (Main Theorem). Consider a Kerr spacetime K(a, m) with |a|/m ≤0.75 and general
solutions11 to the scalar wave equation □a,mψ = N. Then, for any integer s ≥8, 1 + δ < p < 2 −δ, and
any τ1 < τ2, we have

The proof of the Main Theorem 1.1 is based on four steps, which we state as theorems of independent
interest, together with a standard smooth extension argument for the inhomogeneous term N.
Theorem 1.2, which establishes a Morawetz type estimate conditional on the control of the degenerate
energy flux through the horizon and a future spacelike hypersurface, is the core of this paper.
Theorem 1.2 (Morawetz). Under the same assumptions, with s ≥2, we have

easily controlled by T-energy estimates. This, of course, is not the case of Kerr due to the presence of the
ergoregion. The case |a|/m ≪1 can however be treated, as in [18], by replacing T with bT = T +

using the coerciveness of the standard T-energy inequality, that the (deg)E part of the (deg)EF norm of
the right hand side of (1.4) can be replaced by a term of the form

In our work we adopt the opposite point of view, that is we first derive a bound for the global energy
E[ψ] (independent of the flux at the event horizon) and use it to derive the flux estimate, based on a
new application of the AB–invariant operators method. To derive the global energy bound we rely in
an essential way on our new physical space adaptation of Whiting’s integral transformation, mentioned
above. The idea is to transform solutions ψ of the wave equation □gψ = N, with g the Kerr metric, into

requires some mild frequency decomposition (in fact only decomposition into Z-frequency modes). As in
[13] the continuity argument makes use of the orthogonality properties of the Z-frequency modes.

1.5
Main ideas in the proof of Theorems 1.12–1.14
The standard way to prove a Morawetz estimate for solutions of □a,mψ = N is to apply the divergence
theorem in D(τ1, τ2) to an appropriate current P obtained, as in (4.2), based on the energy-momentum
tensor Q of □a,m, a vectorfield X and scalar w. Thus, denoting as in (4.4) (with M = 0), E[ψ][X, w] :=
DµPµ[X, w] −

|q|2E[X, w] = A|∂rψ|2 + P + V|ψ|2,
P = UαβDαψDβψ.
(1.15)
The goal then is to choose an appropriate vectorfield X, proportional to ∂r and scalar w so that, modulo
other divergence terms, A, V are strictly positive and P non-negative. The expectation is that P degen-
erates on the trapping set. Another important consideration is that the boundary terms, generated by
the divergence theorem, be, at least in principle, commensurate with the energy flux generated by the
causal vector-field bT. This imposes serious restrictions on the asymptotic behavior of X and w as r →∞.
For convenience, we refer to these two properties as necessary to achieve partial coerciveness.
We typically choose X = −zfh∂r and w = −z∂r(fh) in which case, see section 4.1,

It can be shown, even in the far simpler case when a = 0, or in the case of axially symmetric solutions,
that partial coerciveness cannot be achieved, unless one either gives up on the smoothness of X and w,
i.e. allow them to blow up at r = r+, or one relies on a modified P = P[X, w, M], depending on an
additional 1-form M, as in (4.2), which is used to rebalance the integrals of I = A|∂rψ|2 and J = V|ψ|2.

In his thesis Stogin introduced a different approach based on the idea of ensuring first the non-negativity
of V, by choosing u = hf appropriately so that z∂ru is constant near the event horizon. The choice
ensures the positivity of A and non-negativity of V, at the price that u blows up logarithmically near
r = r+. To also control the crucial term P, we need to choose u such that it has the same sign as −T
and this can easily be arranged by choosing an appropriate integration constant. Remarkably Stogin’s
method works for the entire sub-extremal case and does not require the Hardy procedure21.
20Following Stogin, we call z a “trapped geodesic potential” in view of the fact that ∂rz vanishes along some trapped
null geodesic.
21However a simpler local Hardy estimate is needed to fix the logarithmic divergence of the vectorfield X at the horizon,

1.5.3
Comments on the Proof of Theorem 1.13
The positivity of the quadratic term in bTψ, ∇ψ in the Morawetz bulk on the left hand side of the estimate
(1.12) corresponds exactly to the positivity of Θ (see (1.1)) away from the trapping set. The proof, given
in section 4.6.1, requires an appropriate Lagrangian modification of the current P, to introduce |Tψ|2 in
the formula for P and a completing the square argument for the corresponding quadratic form, based on
the positivity of Θ in the complement of the trapping set. See remark 4.10 for further details.

Comments on the proof of Theorem 1.14 (low frequency)
We are left with the most technical part of the proof, which consists of estimating the lower order term
Vabψa · ψb. Unfortunately, with the above choice fab, h, the term Vabψa · ψb is not necessarily positive.
In fact, we have23
Vabψa · ψb = Vaψa · Lbψb,
Vaψa = V2T bZψ + V4Oψ.
Due to the presence of the term V2T bZψ, we see that Vabψa · ψb has an indefinite sign (even when V4 is
positive). To handle the term V2T bZψ we make use of the form of the expression of Ψ (see (1.21))
Ψ = 4a
r3 T bZψ + 4a2

in terms of Ψ and the more manageable terms bZ bZψ, Oψ, see Proposition 5.9.
With these observations, multiple integration by parts and a lot of straightforward algebra, we can reduce
to a situation similar to that of the scalar case discussed above around (1.18). More precisely,
I = A|∂r eψ|2,

where A ≥0 and V is only negative in a compact region. To handle the negativity of V, we adopt
the Hardy type procedure discussed in the scalar case. That is, we rely on a modified P = P[X, w, M],
depending on an additional 1-form M = vab∂r, as in (5.1), to rebalance the integrals of I = A|∂r eψ|2 and
J = V| eψ|2. Finally, to recover control of the component TTψ, we need a Lagrangian correction to (1.22).
The detailed proof of Theorem 1.14, given in sections 5 and 6, is technically more involved. This is in
fact the only place in the paper we rely on Mathematica to deal with the algebraic complexity of the
calculations involved.

2.6
Norm equivalence lemmas
In this section we make use of Lemma 2.9 to establish several norm equivalence Lemmas.

Null geodesics in Kerr
In this section, we analyze the behavior of the trapped null geodesics in K(a, m). The characterization
of the r-range of the trapped set given in Proposition 3.8 plays an essential role in the proof of Theorem
1.2.

3.2
Trapped null geodesics
Definition 3.2. Null geodesics γ(λ) which are restricted to a bounded region [r1, r2] of r with r+ < r1 <
r2 < +∞for all values of λ are called trapped. Trapped null geodesics for which r remains constant are
called orbital.
It is known that all trapped null geodesics are in fact orbital, see for example Proposition 2 in [7]. Thus,
from now on, we do not distinguish between trapped and orbital null geodesics.

4.2.2
Main difficulties in the proof of Theorem 1.12
As described in the introduction there are various problems with the choices made above in Proposition
4.4.
1. The vectorfield X behaves like ln(r−r+) bR near the event horizon r = r+. To deal with this singular
behavior we modify X, w (as in [30] and [41]) in section 4.3, by introducing the modified family
Xϵ, wϵ with ϵ > 0 sufficiently small. This introduces a non-positive error term of the form Verror|ψ|2
to the current |q|2E. Note however that the positivity of A and the sign of P are not affected by
this procedure.
2. The coefficient V of ψ2 vanishes for r+ ≤r ≤r3. In fact, given the error term Verror introduced by
the modification of X, w above, it is even slightly negative, proportional to the small parameter ϵ.
This issue is dealt with by using a local Hardy type estimate in section 4.4.
3. The control of | bTψ|2 is missing from the form of P. This, as explained in the introduction, is easily
fixed by adding a Lagrangian term in the Morawetz estimate (see section 4.5.1).
4.3
The tempered pair (Xϵ, wϵ)
In this section, we address the first issue discussed in section 4.2.2.
We define Xϵ and wϵ by changing u so that it is bounded near the event horizon. Let F : R →R be a
smooth function satisfying F(x) = x for x ≤1 and F(x) = 2 for x ≥3. We use F(x) to temper u = hf
by defining
uϵ = ϵ−1F(ϵu),
(4.24)
where ϵ > 0 is a small constant to be chosen later. Then we define
Xϵ = −zuϵ∂r,
wϵ = −z∂ruϵ.
(4.25)
44


## Page 45


We note that Xϵ, wϵ agree with X, w in the region where u < ϵ−1. So Xϵ, wϵ only differ from X, w in the
region where u > ϵ−1, which is a small neighborhood of the event horizon.
Since F(x) ≤2, it follows that uϵ ≤2ϵ−1, which means that for all ϵ > 0, the vectorfield Xϵ is regular
up to the event horizon. Furthermore, since uϵ = 2ϵ−1 near the event horizon, the function wϵ is zero in
a neighborhood of the event horizon, which implies that wϵ has no contribution to the boundary term at
the event horizon (when integrating (4.26) ).
Recall that
|q|2E[Xϵ, wϵ, 0] = Aϵ|∂rψ|2 + Uαβ
ϵ
DαψDβψ + Vϵ|ψ|2
(4.26)

4.4
Control of the lower order term
In this section we will address the second issue discussed in section 4.2.2 concerning the control of the
term ψ2 in the region r ∈[r+, r3]. We fix it by borrowing44 from the |ψ|2 term in the region r ∈[r3, ∞)
to control |ψ|2 in [r+, r3] at the additional cost of an additional term in |∂rψ|2. To achieve this, we need
the control of |e3ψ|2 near the event horizon (since Aϵ|∂rψ|2 ∼r−3∆2|∂rψ|2 only controls |(∆e3 −e4)ψ|2

4.4.2
Local Hardy estimate
Since we finally have good control of |∂rψ|2, we may essentially apply the fundamental theorem of Calculus
to “borrow” from the |ψ|2 term in the region r ∈[r3, ∞) to control |ψ|2 in this region at the additional
cost of some of the |∂rψ|2 term. This technique, which we refer to as the local Hardy estimate, is made
precise by the lemma below.

Remark 6.3. It is in fact the presence of this mixed term eV2 that requires us to restrict the range of our
result to |a|/m ≤0.75.
To deal with this mixed term eV2 we proceed as follows:
• We make use of the trapping quantity P in two different ways (see Proposition 5.9). In the trapping
region, we rewrite eV2T bZψ in terms of P and the more manageable terms TTψ, bZ bZψ, Oψ. Away
from the trapping region, we use P to gain more positivity of TTψ, Oψ. See Propositions 6.5 and
6.6 in section 6.3.
• When |a|/m is relativity small, the way we handle eV3 bZ bZψ (with a2 in eV3) is to write bZ in terms
of T, |q|e2 and then focus on the analysis for TTψ, Oψ. When |a|/m is relatively large, the term
eV3 bZ bZψ is not negligible and we need to treat it separately. See Propositions 6.5 and 6.6 in section
6.3.
Step 3. Finally, we solve the above Riccati inequalties for continuous v1, v3, v4. The details are given in
section 6.4.
Step 4. Since we choose L = (m2, 0, 1, 0) (see (5.36)), the control of | bR≤1Oψ|2 is missing. To handle
this issue, we choose instead L = (m2, 0, 1, σ) where σ is a sufficiently small constant. The details are
given in section 6.5.

the scalar case.

We are now ready to prove the global Morawetz Theorem 1.9 which we rewrite below.
Theorem 8.4. The following global Morawetz estimate61 holds true in the domain D(τ1, ∞) for solutions
of □a,mψ = N, |a|/m ≤0.75, 1 + δ ≤p ≤2 −δ, s ≥8, for which E8

Proof. By a simple orthogonality argument it suffices to prove the result for Z-equivariant solutions.
We thus assume in what follows that Zψ = −iℓψ which allows us to use the estimate (8.4). We then
proceed by a continuity argument, that is we assume that the statement of the Theorem holds true for
